2068. EDWARD BENNETT was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of October, 1 handkerchief, value 4s., the goods of Robert Roberts, from his person.
ROBERT ROBERTS. I am a wine-merchant. On the 14th of October, about half-past six o'clock, I was in St. Paul's church-yard; I felt a tug at my pocket, and a hand in it; I turned round, and saw the prisoner with my handkerchief in his hand; I saw him give it to a person, who ran away with it - I seized the prisoner, and gave him in charge; he resisted, and tried to get away.
Prisoner. Q.Did you not say a young woman told you I had the handkerchief? A. One came up, and said she saw you take it, but I saw it in your hand; he struggled very much, and I lost sight of the woman.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18291029-226]
2069. GEORGE CARMAN was indicted for that he, on the 29th of September, at St. Botolph, without, Bishopsgate, having in his custody and possession, a bill of exchange, which is as follows:- £47: 0: 0 Barming, September 18th, 1829. Two months after date, pay to my order, the sum of forty-seven pounds; value received. GEORGE CARMAN. To Mr. James Ellis, Barming, Kent. upon which said bill of exchange, was written a certain false, forged, and counterfeited acceptance of the said bill of exchange, which said false, forged, and counterfeited acceptance, is as follows:- "Exceped, James Ellis, payable at the George Inn, Boro;" he, the said George Carman, afterwards, on the same day, at the same parish, feloniously did utter, and publish as true, the said forged acceptance, knowing it to be forged, with intent to defraud Isaac Dimsdale Child .
SECOND COUNT, the same, only stating the prisoner's intent to be to defraud James Ellis.
ISAAC DIMSDALE CHILD. I am a wine and spirit-merchant, and live at No. 199 Bishopsgate-street, without; I do not know what trade the prisoner is - he has formerly been a publican; I do not know where he lived - I knew him when he kept a public-house in Old-street road; he called at my house the day before the time in question, and has for some time called at my house, I suppose, every day - there was nothing remarkable in his calling. On Tuesday the 29th of September, about ten o'clock in the morning, he came to me, and said he had a bill, which he wished to have discounted, drawn on Mr. James Ellis, of Barming, in Kent; he stated that he had known James Ellis from a boy, that they were brought up together at school, at Burwash - I asked him if it was Mr. James Ellis, the large hop grower; he said Yes, and that he had street to Mr. Ellis that he was about to take a public-house, and that Mr. Ellis told him he would accommodate him with any sum under 100l.; - that he might draw on him to any amount under 100l.; he then put this bill into my hands - I immediately noticed to him the word "Exceped" across it, instead of accepted; he said that did not matter, the bill was as safe, as the Bank of England, all the same for that, and it did not matter about being altered - he then asked me what money I could let him have immediately on it, as he wanted some immediately to buy some clothes; I told him I was then short of cash, but what I had in the house he should have- he asked me to let him have 2l.; I gave him a sovereign and twenty shillings, and then sent my brother over to the George inn, where the bill was made payable to make the necessary inquiry - my brother had gone before the prisoner went away, but I believe he did not know the bill was out of my hands; I advanced him 10s., more after my brother had left - my brother was gone four hours, or nearly so; the prisoner remained in my house I should think half or three quarters of an hour after my brother was gone - I do not think he knew my brother was gone on this errand, for he went out at the side door; I did not tell him my brother was gone - he came again in the evening, for some more money, I suppose, as I had appointed to let him have more that night or the next morning - on his coming, in consequence of something my brother said I had the prisoner taken.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. What is your brother? A. He has only lately come from sea; he is a seafaring young man - he does not lodge with me; he has been from sea three months or nearly so, and has not employ at present - I am a publican; I have sold horses on commission - I kept livery stables seven years ago; I never dealt in horses with the prisoner; - I advanced the 40s. after the bill was shown to me; I had no previous meeting with him on that day, before the bill was produced - there was nothing to cause any suspicion on my mind about the bill till I saw it, and then the only suspicion was from the word exceped; knowing Mr. Ellis to he a man of opulence I thought he must know how to spell.
Q.Did you not, in the first instance. propose yourself to discount the bill? A. I said I would advance him the money on it - he said he did not want the whole immediately; I was not the first to propose it - he asked if I would advance him a little, and more as he wanted it; I do not think he had seen my brother - he might have seen him in the shop, but he went into the back room; it is very likely he may have seen him on the premises - after he was gone the prisoner asked for 10s. more; I advanced it - he saw my brother in the evening when he was taken; I do not know whether they recognized each other: 50s. is all I advanced on this bill - I have been kind to him in other instance.
DANIEL SPENCER CHILD. I am the prosecutor's brother. I was in the shop when the prisoner came in, but in a minute after I went into the back room; the prisoner saw me - I had seen him before in my brother's shop, drinking liquor; I had been at his house six or sevenyears ago - I had no intimacy with him; (looking at the bill) my brother gave me this bill to make inquiry about it; I went to the George inn, Borough, and in consequence of that inquiry I returned the bill to my brother - the prisoner was not there when I returned: I waited a long time to see Mr. Schofield the proprietor of the George.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you met the prisoner, did not you, afterwards? A. No; I met him out of doors the morning before, and he told me he was going to get a bill which he had got in the Borought, and which he was going to ask my brother to lend him some money on, or to discount it, I cannot say which - I was going into Tooley-street, and walked with him; he did not request me to walk with him - he went with me to a house in Tooley-street, where he drank with me; I did not see him in the after part of the day, till he was taken - my brother's is a gin shop.
COURT. Q.Where did you separate after coming out of the house in Tooley-street? A. Not till we got into Bishopsgate-street again; he turned up a turning in Tooley-street away from me - I waited for him till he returned; he then walked back with me to my brother's.
WESTERMAN SCHOFIELD. I keep the George inn, Borough; there is no other George there - I am acquainted with Mr. James Ellis; he is a large farmer and hop grower - I have known him for years, and am acquainted with his hand-writing perfectly well; I have seen him write, and have known his writing many years (looking at the bill) - I am perfectly satisfied this acceptance is not his hand-writing, nor any thing like it.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you seen him write? A. Yes, for many years, as well as seen his writing; he used my house many years, sometimes two or three times a week - he lives at Barming, in Kent; I know his son who lives in a neighbouring parish - his name is James Day Ellis.
MR. JAMES ELLIS. I have no second Christian name,(Looking at the bill) the name " James Ellis " here is not my hand-writing, nor in the least like it - mine is rather a particular signature; I know no other James Ellis at Barming - my son did live there once, but not at that time: his name is James Johnson Ellis - it is not his writing; I never authorized any one to put my name to such a bill as this - I have no knowledge of the prisoner, and do not recollect that I ever went to school with him; I recollect the name in the parish when I was a boy - I have looked at the prisoner, but do not recollect him; I certainly recollect his brother or a person of his name. but I certainly did not know there was such a man - as far as I have any knowledge I have not known him since I was a boy; I do not remember having been at school with him - if I was I have not kept up any intimacy with him; I did not know there was such a man in existence.
Cross-examined. Q. You are not altogether a stranger to the family; you knew the prisoner? A. I knew there was such a man in the parish of Burwash once, and that his brother has been in my employ; it could easily be discovered that the acceptance was not mine, it is not at all like mine.
COURT. Q. You never authorized the brother to sign your name? A. No.(The bill was here put in and read.)
Prisoner's Defence. (written.) My Lord and Gentlemen of the Jury, - Confident of your patient hearing, I will briefly state the cause which has placed me before you in so critical a situation. My life is in your hands; but I am persuaded, that if any favourable circumstances have transpired during my trial I shall have the benefit of them. The evidence on the part of the prosecution you have heard. I most solemnly declare that I was quite ignorant of the bill being a forged instrument. I was about taking a public house at Deptford; I became possessed of the bill in the following manner: - Smith, formerly a customer of mine, (and now I believe he goes to and fro in the country with fruit) was aware, that I was going into business, he requested me to draw up a bill for two months, and he would get it accepted by a friend of his who would honor it when due; so indifferent was I to what he proposed, that I forgot the circumstance altogether, till, unfortunately for me, I must Smith again: he presented a bill to me drawn for two months, and requested me to put my name, saying he would get it accepted and bring it back again. I did so, and it was brought again to me. My Lord and Gentlemen, is it likely? was it the conduct of a man in his senses to have a bill by me which I knew to be a forgery? and knowing of the fatal consequences resulting from committing the crime of forgery, should I have gone to a person, who has known me for so many years? rather, should I not have applied to have it cashed by a person who would not so easily have detected me; would I, after obtaining 2l. on that bill, have returned to the prosecutor, and that after a lapse of upwards of three hours, having given the said bill into his possession, and receiving 10s. more? I left his house, was absent about half an hour, and returned once more to his house, when I was apprehended on a charge of forgery, without the least inquiry being made as to whether the person who was said to have accepted the bill was a responsible person or not. My Lord and Gentlemen, I do not hesitate to say, that it was a vile conspiracy against me for my destruction. I will briefly state the circumstances, and leave the rest to your deliberate consideration. I called at the prosecutor's house and had something to drink; the prosecutor then asked me what he should lend me on that bill, as he could do some good with it. I told him I did not immediately want money; he however persuades me to take 2l., and sends me after the bill - on my road to fetch it, his brother way-lays me, and never leaves me till I am taken into custody. My Lord and Gentlemen, I beseech you most earnestly, that as my reputation amongst society, nay, my life is now at stake, and entirely depending on your verdict, I feel confident that you will give it every consideration that the nature of my case requires; and I aver, that which ever way this investigation may terminate, I am entirely innocent of the crime laid to my charge. I feel assured, after a candid and conscientious inquiry into the evidence, you will return a verdict in favour of the unfortunately person who now addresses you.
MR. CHILD. I know the prisoner's hand-writing; the signature
" George Carman " to the bill is his writing.
Seven witnesses gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 45.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury, on account of his good character.
[trialAccount t18291029-227]
2070. HENRY MORTIMER was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of September, at Christchurch, 2 silver watches, value 6l.; 1 seal, value 10s., and 1 gold ring, value 2l., the goods of James Drake, in his dwelling-house.
JAMES DRAKE. I live in Crispin-street, Spitalfields, in the parish of Christchurch, and am a watch-gilder; I keep the house. The prisoner came to me as a lodger on the 26th of September - he had one room on the second floor,as a single man; I keep my watches in drawers on the ground floor. On the 30th of September, about two o'clock, I went to see what the time was, and found every thing correct in the drawer; I shut the drawer and the door of the room, but did not lock it - I saw the prisoner in the shop between four and five o'clock; he said he wanted to write a letter to send to Portsmouth, to his father - I keep a chandler's-shop; the watches were in my bed-room- the prisoner had slept at home every night till then, but did not on that night; I went to the drawer about half-past seven o'clock that evening, and missed two silver watches, they were fairly worth from 3l. to three guineas each, at least, and a chain to each watch - a gold mourning-ring, worth from 38s., to 2l. - I gave 38s. for it at a jeweller's shop; it weighed 5 1/2 dwts., and a seal worth 10s.; in consequence of the prisoner saying he was writing to his father at Portsmouth. I went down there about the 17th or 18th of October, and found him at his father's house: I gave him into custody, and had him brought here - (looking at a watch produced by Wild) this is one of the watches I lost, and the chain to it; I have had the watch about eighteen months; I am not the maker - it is my own gilding; I am quite sure of it - there is no maker's name on it; it was in this state when I lost it.
ROBERT WILD. I am shopman to Mr. Sowerby, a pawnbroker, of Chiswell-street. I received this watch in pawn from the prisoner on the 30th of September, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, and lent him 30s. on it; I never saw him before, but am quite certain of his person - I gave him what I supposed amounted to 30s.; he came next morning, and said I had given him a 7s. piece instead of a half-sovereign - I said, "I gave it you for the watch you pawned last night, did not I?" he said Yes, and I gave him half a sovereign for it - I am quite certain of him.
JOSHUA ARMSTRONG. I am an officer of Worship-street. I went to Portsmouth, and took the prisoner.
Prisoner. Armstrong knows my character. Witness. His father has been forty-two years in His Majesty's dockyard at Portsmouth, and has been a very respectable man.
Prisoner. My friends are all at Portsmouth.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 22.
[trialAccount t18291029-228]
Before Mr. Justice Park.
2071. WILLIAM CLEGG was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of September, at Paddington, 1 watch, value 20l; 1 seal, value 1l.; 1 brooch, value 10s.; 4 rings, value 2l.; 1 pair of sugar-tongs, value 10s.; 1 spoon, value 10s., and 40 herrings, value 5s., the goods of Alexander Cabbell Mann, in his dwelling-house.
CAROLINE COURTNEY MANN. I am the wife of Alexander Cabbell Mann, who is a fishmonger - we live in Connaught-terrace, Edgware road, in the parish of Paddington. The prisoner was about three weeks in our employ - he had been in service before he came to us; he did not sleep in our house, but came in the day time. On the 4th of September he came as usual a little after seven o'clock; I let him in at the private door, and desired him to go down into the kitchen and light the fire - I went down into the kitchen with him, and the watch was hanging over the dresser; it was a gold watch - I had hung it there a quarter of an hour before - I left him in the kitchen, and went down again to see the time at twenty minutes to eight o'clock, and the watch hung there then, and he was there; I went up stairs to call my husband and children, and when I came down he had opened the shop door, and was gone; I had told him not to open the shop door - I missed the watch, also a gold brooch from up stairs in the bed-room; he could not have taken that that morning, but it was safe the morning before; I also missed four rings from a drawer in the bedroom, and a silver table-spoon and sugar-tongs from the same place; there was a seal to the watch: about forty herrings were taken from the shop - I had seen them that morning: I have never seen the things since - I inquired about at the pawnbrokers in the neighbourhood. I never saw the prisoner again till the 23rd of October, when I found him in a cart at Kensington gravel-pits; my husband was with me, and took him.
GEORGE WATTS. I know the prisoner - I have seen him about Kensington gravel-pits; I first saw him about the 7th of September, and have played about there with him - I have seen him with two or three sovereigns; we used to ask him how he got them, and he said he lived at a bonnet-shop in Regent-street three years - that his master never paid him regularly, and when he did he paid him as much as seven sovereigns.
MRS. MANN re-examined. He came into our service from Mr. Plummer, a hosier, of Regent-street, but we took him on his mother's recommendation, and did not see Plummer. The watch was gold, and worth 20l.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 15.

Before Mr. Baron Garrow.
2072. JOSEPH MOGGERIDGE was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of James Ward, on the 14th of October, and stealing 2 jugs, value 1s. 6d.; 2 dishes, value 9d.; 4 plates, value 8d.; 2 saucers, value 2s., and 21 penny-pieces, his property.
MARGARET WARD. I am nine years old, and live with my father, James Ward, in Whitecross-street. On the 14th of October I was alone in the house, I shut the street door, left it on the latch, and went out into the back yard; I remained there about a quarter of an hour, then came back, and saw a little boy run out - I did not see whether he had any thing, now hear any noise; I had time to see him; I had never seen him before - I missed some Delphware from the cupboard, and some halfpence in a little bag, which was in a mug - I went and told my mother almost directly; she was in Whitecross-street, selling fruit: I then got a constable, and went to Moggeridge's father's house, which is next door but one to us - the constable knocked at the door; it was not opened - he got in at the window, and let me in; this was within ten minutes of the time, but nothing was found - the prisoner is not the boy who ran out; he was much less than him: he told the Magistrate the door was kept fast because his father expected an execution; I am quite sure he is not the boy, for I knew the prisoner.
NOT GUILTY.

JAMES ISAAC. I saw the prisoner running up Hatton-wall - I came up to him, and he was going to throw this waistcoat down an area; he was about three hundred yards from Solomon's - I took him to the watch-house; the prosecutor came, and said if it was his there was a figure of 8 on the collar, and there is one; the prisoner said he found it in the middle of Great Saffron-hill.(Property produced and sworn to.)

2075. WILLIAM SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of October, 1 shawl, value 3s., the goods of Richard Smale.
RICHARD SMALE. I keep a coffee-room. On the 24th of October my wife came in and threw this shawl across the wainscot, and went up stairs; the prisoner was there, and called for a cup of coffee - he drank part of it, and went out suddenly; in less than five minutes my wife missed her shawl: on Friday last she saw him in the street, and brought him home - I questioned him about it; he at first denied it - I told him I thought it was his first offence, and he did it through distress, and if he could tell me where I could go for a good character, I would be as lenient as I could with him - he said he did it through distress, and he worked at a dyer's near Finsbury-square.
LEONARD CLARE MATTHEWS. I have a shawl, pawned on Saturday week, by the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I took it up, as I thought there had been some woman in the place; the prosecutor said if I would give it up, he would not appear against me.
GUILTY. Aged 18.

2076. SUSAN SURETEES was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of October, 3 pairs of leather soles, value 1s. 3d., the goods of Eusebius Smith.
JOHN ANDREW COTTON. I am shopman to Mr. Eusebias Smith, a leather-cutter, of Kingsland-road. Last Saturday the prisoner came and bought a pair of soles, which she paid for, then stooped down, and put these three pairs into her basket, and then the pair she had bought - she was going out; I told her Mr. Smith wanted a word with her, and I rang the bell for Mr. Smith - she found there was something amiss, and went to take the soles out of the basket; she took out two pairs; I said."That will not do - you must let me look into the basket; "Mr. Smith said he would send for an officer, for she had been doing it some time; these three pairs of soles were taken from her basket - she said she was very sorry.
GEORGE SMITH. I am a constable. I went to the shop- the prisoner was delivered to me, with these three pairs of soles; she begged forgiveness.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I deny putting any into the basket - I looked out some, and gave the witness to put by for me till I returned; I had not put them into the basket, and he knows it well.
GUILTY. Aged 46.
Confined Six Months.

2078. BURDETT ROGERS was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of August, 1 sovereign, 1 half-sovereign, and 1 shilling, the monies of George Morgan.
GEORGE MORGAN. I live at a club-house in St. James'-street - the prisoner was employed there as a painter's labourer. I came home about five o'clock on the 24th of August, and asked him to clean my shoes; I took off my trousers, with this money in them, and put them on a box in the dressing-room - I went down stairs, and in about five minutes I came up again, to take the money out of the trousers; I then missed upwards of 30s.: my fellow-servant came in, and I sent for the prisoner - I asked him if he had picked up this money; he denied it - I told him I would make him pull all his things off; he still denied it, and my fellow-servant went for an officer - while he was gone, the prisoner said if I would not hurt him he would give me the money; he took some of it out; I said that was not all - he then took out the rest from his inside trousers pocket, and laid it down; I went out for a few minutes - when I returned he was gone; he was taken soon after, for something else.
Prisoner's Defence. When the prosecutor came home that day he was quite intoxicated - I was called to go on an errand, and when I returned I went to finish the shoes; I let one of the brushes fall, and in picking it up I saw the money - I should have given it him, but he had some friends at tea. GUILTY. Aged 17.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury. - Confined 1 Month.

2079. WILLIAM COURTNEY was indicted for that he, on the 20th of October, 50lbs. weight of lead, value 6s., the goods of George William Meriton, and fixed to a building of his, feloniously did rip, cut, and break, with intent to steal the same; against the Statute.
SAMUEL COCK. I am an exciseman. The house in question is in Ann-street, Bethnal-green, and belongs to George William Meriton; I collect the rent for him - he lives in the country; I saw the house unroofed last Saturday, but I did not go on the roof - I had seen it safe about a fortnight before.
JOSEPH CASSELL. I am a headborough of Bethnal-green. One Wednesday night I met the watchman, and went with him and the inspector to Ann-street - the door of this house was fastened; it is not inhabited - we got through another house and into that; we looked into a cupboard in a corner, and saw a hole in the cicling; we got into the hole but found nothing - the watchman found the prisoner on the roof.
JOHN PIERCY. I am a watchman. I went to the house, and got through the hole on the roof; I saw the prisoner dragging his legs from the ceiling to get on the roof - I went and took him, and found this knife laying by his side in the gutter - the lead had been cut and turned up for three or four feet; I charged the prisoner with it - he said there was no one there then, but the other had made his escape out of a window - and I heard a window thrown up at the back of the premises; the prisoner said he got up there to lodge - there was nothing on him but a small penknife.
GEORGE CLAY. I am an inspector of the watch. I went with the witnesses and took the prisoner - it was about half-past twelve o'clock; I found this penknife on him.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been at work for Abraham Montague till half-past nine o'clock, and then went drinking - and how I got on the house I do not know.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Transported for Seven Years.

2081. BENJAMIN HANTON was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of October, two phosphorus-boxes, value 3d.; 1 newspaper, value 2d.; 2 snuff-boxes, value 1s.; 2 jars, value 4d., and 1 book, value 6d., the goods of Henry Francis Piaget.
MR. LEE conducted the prosecution.
HENRY FRANCIS PIAGET. I keep a circulating library and a tobaconist's shop - the prisoner was my servant. On Friday evening, the 16th of October, I went to my tea-caddy and found the prisoner's hat on it - I took it off and felt it was very heavy; I lifted up his handkerchief, which was in it, and found a phosphorus-box and a newspaper - I sent him up stairs, and called in a person who saw what was in it, and we sent for Mr. Clark; in about half an hour we searched his box, which he opened himself, and found in it one phosphorus-box, two snuff-boxes, two snuff-canisters, and a book, which are mine - these are the articles.
Cross-examined by MR. COLLVER. Q. The prisoner expressed no reluctance to open his box? A. No; he did it by order of Mrs. Clark, his mistress - he is not my apprentice.(Property produced and sworn to.)
JOHN CLARK. I was in the shop while they went up stairs to open the box; the prisoner said he did not know how he came by the things; he knew they were Mr. Piaget's.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you said any thing to him about being prosecuted? A. No; I told him as I had found him out in so many things, I would certainly go to the full extent of the law; he was apprenticed to me, but used to do a few things for the prosecutor, who is single; he has been a good boy till lately, when he has got some bad companions.
EDWARD PAYNE. I am an officer. I took the prisoner.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Whipped and Discharged.

2083. JOSEPH KINGSTON was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of October, 24 pieces of tape, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Jonathan Orme.
MARY LEE. I am a shopwoman to Mr. Jonathan Orme, a linen-draper, of Russell-street, Covent-garden. On the 28th of October the prisoner came in to purchase some webbing for boots - he is a shoemaker; while the personwho was serving him went to get it, he took twenty-four pieces of tape off the counter and put them into his pocket: I told a boy to go and tell Mr. Orme, and just as the prisoner got to the door he stopped him.
Prisoner. It was outside the counter I picked them up. Witness. No; he reached over the counter and took them.
ROBERT ORME. I am the son of Jonathan Orme. Lee sent to me, and as the prisoner was going out I told him he had got a pound of thread, which she said she thought it was; I took him into the passage - he pulled out this tape, begged my pardon, and said he did not mean to keep it; he has come to the shop for three or four years.
Prisoner. I beg for mercy; I have a dying mother and two small sisters at home whom I support.
GUILTY. Aged 35.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18291029-241]
2084. JOHN HEWITT was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of October, 1 saw, value 6s., the goods of William Wells.
WILLIAM WELLS. I lost a saw last Friday from a building at which I was at work in Copenhagen-street, between twelve o'clock and a quarter before one, while I was gone to dinner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
ZECHARIAH PEELE. I am a carpenter. I was working at the same place; the prosecutor's wife called me to look after a person who was coming out of the building; I went and saw the prisoner coming out and going away - I asked him what he wanted in the building; he said he was looking for a job; I saw he had something under his coat, and drew out this saw.
Cross-examined by MR. COLLYER. Q. Was this in the house? A. No, in the street.
WILLIAM BROWN EDWARDS. I took the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence I was looking for work, and saw this building not finished - there was no carpenter's bench nor tools, but this saw was on the floor; I thought some carpenter had left it by mistake, and I took it to take to the public-house to inquire what carpenter had been at work - the witness came and asked what I meant by stealing the saw: I said I meant to take it to a public-house.
WILLIAM WELLS. I constantly work there - we generally leave our tools on the bench; they were not hid - I was at work up stairs - my bench was on the first floor.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury - Confined Seven Days.
[trialAccount t18291029-242]
2085. MARGARET BYRNE was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of October, 1 pair of spectacles, value 10s.; 1 ring, value 10s.; 3 keys, value 1s. 6d.; 1 pair of gloves, value 6d.; 1 cap, value 2d.; 1 pocket, value 1d.; 1 bonnet, value 2d.; 1 knife, value 1d.; 1 snuff-box, value 1d.; 1 pencil-case, value 1d., and 3 thimbles, value 1/2d., the goods of James Selling Hewkley.
JAMES SELLING HEWKLEY. I missed my wife on the 25th of October - I went to look for her, and found her in Bunhill-row watch-house; the officer had got these articles - I knew them all to be mine, and they had been in my wife's possession.
JOHN BROWN. On Saturday, the 24th of October, the prisoner was brought to Bunhill-row watch-house in a state of intoxication - soon after the prosecutor's wife was brought in, and was placed with the prisoner; she was also intoxicated - the prosecutor came in the course of the night to inquire for his wife; we went into the place, and found his wife laying dead and the prisoner was rising from her person - we took the prisoner, and found all these articles on her, which the prosecutor has identified.
THOMAS WALKER. I was in the watch-house when the deceased was brought in; I saw the prisoner there - these articles were found on her - I believe she did not say any thing; I found this bonnet on her head - I took her to Finsbury watch-house in the morning.
HENRY HARRINGTON. I received charge of the prisoner. I found this ring on her finger, which she said was her own property, and this duplicate which had belonged to the deceased.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was so intoxicated I did not know what I was doing.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.

2086. JOHN SHELLS was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of September, 3 sovereigns, and 19 shillings, the monies of David Robinson Watts, his master.

ROBERT MURRAY. I am a shopman to Mr. Laing, of the Haymarket. The prisoner brought the bottles of liquor; Mr. Laing took them, and asked him if he could give him change for a 5l. note - he said No; Mr. Laing then gave him four sovereigns, and he gave him 1s.
HENRY RICHARD BUCKERIDGE. I found the prisoner in Fitzroy-market on the 15th of September; he said he had got tipsy and lost the money, which I believe was the case.

2087. JOSEPH WILKINS was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of October, 1 watch, value 4l.; 1 seal, value 5s.; 1 watch-chain, value 1s., and 1 watch-key, value 1d. the goods of John Ingram, from his person.
JOHN INGRAM. I am a camp equipage maker. I was at the Ship public-house at Charing-cross, on the 28th of October; I was not very sober - I left there at five minutes past eight o'clock: when I left, two soldier s came to me and offered to walk with me, and did walk with me to the end of Parliament-street, on my way home to Lambeth - I said I was much abliged to them, and if they chose to take any thing they should have it; we went into the Horse Shoe and Magpie in Bridge-street, and had some rum; I then went on, and soon after I saw the same two soldiers behind me - they came up, looked very hard in my face, and one of them took my watch; it was gone in a moment - I pursued them down the steps into Palaceyard, but I slipped down, sprained my ancle, and could not pursue further; I believe the prisoner is the man who took my watch.
JOHN WHARTON. I am a shopman to Mr. Page, apawnbroker in Vauxhall-bridge-road. The prisoner pawned this watch with me on Friday last; I gave him this duplicate.
JAMES ROGERS. I am a servant at the Horse Shoe and Magpie. I saw the prosecutor at our house last Wednesday night rather tipsy; the prisoner and another soldier were with him, and they drank together.
FERDINAND JOHNS. I am a constable. On Friday night last I saw the prisoner intoxicated in the street - I took him into custody, and found this duplicate on him; the prosecutor had given notice of this robbery at our watch-house - he said it was a young man in a military undress.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was at a house in Brownlow-street; I left a quarter before nine o'clock, and came straight down to the barracks - I had not been at that part of Westminster the whole day. On Friday a man in a red jacket came to me and said, "Soldier, I will sell you a bargain;" he took me aside, showed me this watch, and said he would sell it for 1l. - I said I had not 1l., but I would give him 15s. for it, as I was no judge of watches; I had never had one in my life - I bought it, and then, as I had to go on furlough, I pawned it to get the money.
ROBERT DUFF. I am pay-serjeant of the company to which the prisoner belongs, in the 3rd regiment of Foot Guards - he was present at the roll call in the King's`mews barracks, at nine o'clock on Wednesday night last; he has been above four years in the regiment, and had a good character for honesty.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18291029-245]
2088. JAMES WARD was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of October, 1 handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of Joseph Dudley Webb, from his person.
JOSEPH DUDLEY WEBB. On Friday last, between four and five o'clock, I was going up Queen's Head-lane, Islington; three boys followed me, and the eldest, who was this prisoner, pushed against me, and looked me in the face - I put my hand to my pocket, and my handkerchief was gone; the boy had then got on five or six yards before me - I cried Stop thief! ran on, and seized him; I said, "Where is my handkerchief?" he said, "I have not got it, search me;" a youg girl, who stood at a door, said," Your handkerchief is on those steps" - I dragged him back, and took it up; he had passed the others, but they were behind me when I missed my handkerchief; it must have been thrown there before he passed me; I had seen him with two others for a minute or two before.
ELIZABETH HARDING. I live in Queen's Head-lane. I saw this gentleman there; the prisoner picked his pocket, and threw the handkerchief on the steps - I saw it done, and told the gentleman of it; he took up the same handkerchief the prisoner threw down.
JAMES ROOMES. I am a watchman of Islington. I took the prisoner, and gave him to Mr. Cope the officer.
THOMAS COPE. I received the prisoner, and this handkerchief.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. There were two boys behind me, and I walked on before the gentleman - he then came, took me, and said, "You have my handkerchief;" I said I had not - the other two boys ran away; the little girl and a gentleman, who was there, said it was not me.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Transported for Life.

Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
2089. WILLIAM WILSON was indicted for embezlement.
JOHN BARHAM. I am a baker, and live at the corner of Baker-street, New-road - the prisoner had been four weeks with me as a weekly servant; I gave him 12s. a week - he was to take out bread, and to receive money and account for it every afternoon: I was not satisfied with him, and gave him notice to quit - he left me on Saturday, and on Monday I discovered this.
FRANCES DELL. I deal with the prosecutor - I paid the prisoner on a Thursday or Friday, about the 25th of September, 15s. for his master; it was a weekly bill - he wrote this receipt on it (read); I gave him a half-sovereign, two half-crowns, or 5s.
JOHN BARHAM. He did not bring me this money; he said they wanted change for a sovereign, and he could not give it.
Prisoner's Defence. I received the money, and paid it to the shopkeeper - the prosecutor was there at the time; he stopped 5s., and said he would make a debt of it.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.

2090. WILLIAM MESSENGER was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of September, 8 feet of deal plank, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Richard Chapman; and that at the delivery of the King's Gaol of Newgate, holden for the County of Middlesex, at Justice-hall, on Wednesday, the 25th of October, in the 56th year of the reign of George III, he was convicted of felony by the name of William Passenger.
JOHN HARDY. I am a watchman of Islington. At a quarter-past four o'clock in the morning of the 13th of September, I met the prisoner in the back road with this piece of timber under his arm - I said, "Mate, what is this?" he dropped it, and offered me 1s. 6d. to let him go, but I would not; he was then within a few hundred yards of the prosecutor's.
RICHARD CHAPMAN, JUN. This was my father's property; his name is Richard. I had seen it safe in the brick field, but I cannot say when; the prisoner had been in our employ three years, but when we found he was a returned transport we discharged him, and since he has been in our employ we have lost twenty-two planks.
Prisoner's Defence. I took this bit of plank by order of the foreman to lay over a dirty ditch, for some of my children to get a penny if they could; the foreman said I might have it if I brought it back at night.
WILLIAM ELLIS. I produce a copy of the conviction of the prisoner by the name of William Passenger (read).
WILLIAM GALE. I am a timber merchant. I remember the prisoner being tried on that indictment; I attended on the part of my employer - I have no doubt of his being the man.
GUILTY. Aged 45.
Transported for Life.

2094. ABEL PIERS was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of September, 1 silver tea-pot, value 4l., the goods of William Cathcart Boyd.
SECOND COUNT, stating it to be the property of Louisa Elizabeth Boyd.
DANIEL FORRESTER. On the 25th of September I saw the prisoner and another turn out of Moorfields-square and go, one to South-place and the other to South-street; I went down Cross-street and came up South street - I missed the prisoner, but saw the other man; I went on to Wilson-street, saw the prisoner come up to Wilson-street, and then he ran - I went and took him, and found this tea-pot in his apron.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Did you see the two persons together? A. Yes, walking within a yard of each other; I did not observe any conversation - I think they were not walking in the usual way; the prisoner did not come to me and ask what I wanted - I took hold of his collar; he said he had been to Blackfriars, and found the tea-pot - the other man did not join him afterwards; I took him about thirty yards from the prosecutor's.
CATHERINE JONES. I live servant with Mrs. Louisa Elizabeth Boyd, at No. 2, South-street. This tea-pot is her's, and was either on the kitchen table, or in the pantry on the 25th of September; the person who took it must have come down the area steps.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you put it at night? A. I used to take it to my bed-room - I am sure I had it in my bed-room the night before, and brought it down that morning; the Lion on the top of it turns round, and I have it in use every day.
ELIZA MILLER. I am a niece of Mrs. Boyd's; she is married to William Cathcart Boyd.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been over the water to receive orders, and coming down Moorfields I saw a handkerchief on the step of a door - I took it up and put it into my apron - I had not got more than twenty yards, when the officer said, "What have you there?" I went back, and said, "A tea-pot;" and gave it him.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Transported for Seven Years.

WILLIAM REYNOLDS. I went to the prisoner's lodgings in King-street, Drury-lane, about three o'clock on Sunday; he was not there - I went again at two in the morning; he was in bed; he said he had it, but it was a mistake.
Prisoner's Defence. I took it by mistake.

2096. ANN COLLINS, MARY TEWSBURY, and JOHN NEAL, were indicted for stealing, on the 31st of October, 1 purse, value 9d.; 3 half-crowns, 4 shillings, and 2 sixpences, the property of William Rickarby, from his person.

WILLIAM JEFFREYS. I saw the prosecutor and the three prisoners come into the Castle tavern, Holborn - they called for half a pint of liquor - the prosecutor took out his purse and paid for it - he had change for half a crown, and put 1s. 6d. into his purse again; his purse had some other silver which looked like half-crowns in it - he put it into his left-hand pocket - I saw Collins put her hand into his pocket and take out his purse; the other two prisoners were sitting opposite her, they saw it, and winked at her - I told the officer, who took Collins, and found the purse on her - they were all taken before they left the box.
-. I am an officer. I took this purse from Collins.(Property produced and sworn to.)

2097. RICHARD MILLER was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of October, 1 dead goose, value 4s., the goods of Edward Tidyman.
CHARLES HERBERT. I am a Police-constable. On the 31st of October, about three minutes after ten o'clock at night, I saw the prisoner a quarter of a mile from the prosecutor's - he was running, and carrying a dead goose; I ran after him, and saw he had but one shoe on - I asked what he had got; he said a piece of meat; I asked him to let me look, and saw it was a goose; I asked where hegot it; he said if I would go home with him to the New-road he would tell me; I said it was too far - he then said it was given him; I took him to the watch-house, and found the prosecutor there, who claimed it - the prisoner was very much in liquor.
CHARLES TIDYMAN. I am a poulterer, and live in Hand-court. I missed a goose from my window - I found it at the watch-house.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought it of a woman for 4s. 6d.; I was very tipsy.
GUILTY. Aged 32.

2100. MARGARET BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of November, 1 hat, value 4s., the goods of John Young, from his person.
JOHN YOUNG. I am a mariner. On Sunday morning, about one o'clock, the prisoner took my hat off my head. and ran away with it; I had not walked with her before - wanted me to do so, but I would not; the watchman took me to the watch-house, and showed it to me.
JOHN ANDERSON. I am a watchman. I met the prisoner about half-past two o'clock in the morning, in Shakespeare-walk: I asked where she was going - she said home- I asked what she had in her apron: she said Nothing - I found this hat there: she then said she took it out of pawn for 1s. 6d. - this was about two hundred yards from where the prosecutor was robbed.
JURY. Q. Was he in liquor? A. He had had a drop of drink, but was not intoxicated - I found him at three o'clock, and said, "Where is your hat?" he said a woman took it off his head, and ran away with it.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I never saw the gentleman before- I found the hat in the kennel in Fox's-lane.
GUILTY. Aged 45.

2101. SARAH RICHARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of October, 1 scarf, value 5s., the goods of Ann Freak, widow, from her person.
ANN FREAK. I am a widow. On the 5th of October I was at the corner of Monmouth-street, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening, and felt my shawl taken off my shoulder - I said, "For God's sake, don't take my shawl;" I saw the prisoner near me: I saw no more of my shawl till I saw it at the Magistrate's the same evening - two officers came up, and assisted me.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. Q. Did you ask me to lead you out of the public-house? A. I am not saying contrary to that.
Prisoner. She threw her shawl off when she was sitting on the step of a door, and was sick from the effects of the liquor - she told me to take care of it - I was holding her when the officer came up.
JOHN THURTELL. I was on my beat that evening, and saw this old lady, who had had a drop, but was not incapable of taking care of herself; the prisoner was with her - she set her down, and then drew the shawl from off her back, and tucked it under her hams; I said to my serjeant,"Do you see that?" he said Yes; I went and asked the prisoner to assist me up with the old lady; she said she was not able; I got her up, and the prisoner took the shawl from her hams, and went away; my serjeant stopped her.
PHILIP RILEY. I am serjeant of the Police. I saw the prisoner shifting the shawl under her - I said to her, "Why don't you assist the woman?" she said, "I am very bad, and can't;" she went away with the shawl, and I stopped her. She and another sat on the step - the old lady sat on the flag-stones; the prisoner had got as far as the public-house when I stopped her.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18291029-259]
2102. JOSEPH GODFREY was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of November, 3lbs. weight of bacon, value 1s. 9d., the goods of Edward Lees and Thomas Russ.
EDWARD LEES. I am in partnership with Thomas Russ - we are cheesemongers. Last night, between six and seven o'clock, I had seen the prisoner lurking about, and a friend observed him take this bacon; I followed him, and asked what he was going to do with it - he said it was not mine; I took it from him.
GEORGE CAVE. I was in Mr. Watson's shop, and saw the prisoner in the adjoining passage; I saw him put his arm round into the prosecutor's shop, but I did not see him take the bacon - I saw him for about ten minutes, while I was washing myself: when I got out he was in custody.
WILLIAM PORTEOUS. I was on duty, and a man told me I was wanted - I went, and the prosecutor gave charge of the prisoner, for taking this bacon; he made a violent resistance, and offered 2s. a lb. for the bacon.
EDWARD LEES. This is my bacon - it fell from my hand when I took it from under the prisoner's coat; it could not have been reached without a person stepped into my shop.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not take it from the shop, and he did not take it from me - he at first said he found it in the street.
GUILTY. Aged 52.
Confined Three Weeks.
[trialAccount t18291029-260]
2103. JOHN HUTTON was indicted for stealing, onthe 2nd of November, 1 pint of brandy, value 2s., the goods of the London-dock company.
WILLIAM OSBORN. I am a London-dock watchman. I caught the prisoner in the act of broaching a cask of brandy, which laid on the quay, about half-past one o'clock yesterday; I took this bottle of brandy out of his trousers - it has about a pint in it; the prisoner was employed there - there were a good many people about; he had bored a hole, and put a spile in - I asked him to give up his tool, and he gave this gimblet.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q.Was his back to you? A. When he saw me he turned his back to the wall- his back was to me when he was doing something at the cask; the cask was leaking from the hole - the bottle was in his breeches; I cannot say that the contents came from the cask - it appeared to be a brandy cask; I did not taste the contents of it - I did not say yesterday that it was rum - I have not tasted what is in this bottle: it smells like brandy - the cask was in the custody of the dock company.
COURT. Q. Did you taste any of that which came from the hole? A. No - a brandy cask is of a different model to others: it was a sort of cask that brandy is generally brought in - it was on the wharf of the London-dock company, and the prisoner was in their employ.
CHARLES WEBB. I am a cellar-clerk in the spirit-quay of the London-docks. I saw the cask which Osborn pointed out; I dipped it, and found it deficient about one quart - it was brandy, and of the same description as this in the bottle - I tasted them.
Cross-examined. Q. What difference in a cask would one quart make? A. About 2-10ths in the rule; there is not a quart in this bottle, but the cask was leaking - I do not know whether any one else had been there; to the best of my judgment the brandy in the cask and this in the bottle are the same - I have no hesitation at all in swearing that: the prisoner was an extra man there - all persons are searched coming out, and I should think he would have been stopped with it; no sample bottles are allowed there.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not bore the cask - I took the spile out.
One witness gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 27.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury, believing it to he his first offence.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18291029-261]
2104. JOHN ROBERTS was indicted for feloniously assaulting James Wilbey, with intent to rob him.
JAMES WILBEY. The day before yesterday I was going home to Walthamstow, and called on my daughter at Newington. I and my wife had two pints of porter at the Three Crowns - we left at half-past seven o'clock in the evening; I was perfectly sober: when we got to Tile-kiln-lane, I met three men - I saw them before they came up to us; the prisoner was one of them, and he had a pack at his back - a man on his right-hand struck me, and I fell; in getting up the prisoner's hand was close against my watch; I called out Watch! my wife then said she was struck, and I cried Murder! the men made the best of their way off - I am certain the prisoner was one, and he was in the middle; it was star-light - the prisoner went forty or fifty yards, then came back, and said, "What the devil is the matter?" we called Watch! Watch! he ran, and called Watch! Picket came up, and he had hold of the prisoner; I said that was the very man, and he had been in the centre of the three, with a pack at his back - he is a bird-catcher; neither of the others had a pack - I saw his face quite plain; the officer took charge of him, and he was taken to the watch-house - I did not lose my watch; he put his hand against it, but the seals were in my clothes, and there was nothing for any one to lay hold of.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. He was quite a stranger? A. Yes; there was a lamp in the lane - there were no trees near us; I swear to him both by the fact, and the man himself - I was not muddled; the man who ran away, called Watch! the man who struck against my watch, went away, and came back - he did not say any thing, till he came back; no hand had been thrust into my fob.
MARTHA WILBEY. I am the prosecutor's wife. In Tile Kiln-lane, we met three men; they knocked my husband's hat off - the prisoner put his hand to my husband's watch; he had a pack on his back - I saw it as I picked up my husband's hat; I said, "How dare you insult my husband, he said nothing to you;" one of the others then struck me in the eye.
Cross-examined Q. Had you been with your husband all the afternoon? A. Yes, and drank a little, but not to hurt me - I drank at a grocer's shop, but not at the Three Crowns, only two pints were drank there - I saw a large bunch of onions.
WILLIAM PICKET. I am an officer. I went into Tile Kiln-lane, on my way home; I met three or four men running, who said, "They want you - a man and woman have been insulted down the lane; have you met a man?" I said Yes - they said, "Had he any thing on his back?" I said No - I ran down the hill, and saw the prosecutor; his wife was holding her handkerchief to her eye - I said,"Which way did he go?" they said, "Down the hill;" I ran and overtook the prisoner, who was walking towards the bridge - I said, "You are my prisoner;" he said he was willing to go with me, but he knew nothing of it - the prosecuter said he was the man who tried to take his watch; the prisoner had some onions, and a large package of bird-cages - the prosecutor said he was one of the three that had insulted him, and had put his hand to his watch
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not say he was the man who clapped his hand to his watch? A.Something like that; the prisoner had a large load - he had some sticks, or poles, and some nets; that is a very common place for bird-catchers on Sundays - there had been several parties of bird-catchers that day.
JAMES WILBEY re-examined. Q. Were your watch and seals entirely concealed? A. Yes; no one could take hold of it - he put his hand against it; he did not strike me in any other way - I told Henry Watts how we had been used, and he told the officer.
Prisoner's Defence. I got to the high bridge, one hundred or two hundred and fifty yards before the prosecutor - I heard the woman cry out; I turned back and said, "Mistress, what is the matter?" I then went on, but I had lost a little dog, and went back to see for him - I then turned to go home, and met the officer.
NOT GUILTY.
